Global Calderón-Zygmund theory for fractional Laplacian type equations Thanks: S. Byun was supported by NRF-2022R1A2C1009312, K. Kim was supported by IRTG 2235/NRF-2016K2A9A2A13003815 and D. Kumar was supported by NRF-2021R1A4A1027378.
Sun-Sig Byun, Kyeong Bae Kim, Deepak Kumar

Abstract
. We establish several fine boundary regularity results of weak solutions to non-homogeneous s s -fractional Laplacian type equations. In particular, we prove sharp Calderón-Zygmund type estimates of u / d s u/d^{s} depending on the regularity assumptions on the associated kernel coefficient including VMO, Dini continuity or the Hölder continuity, where u u is a weak solution to such a nonlocal problem and d d is the distance to the boundary function of a given domain. Our analysis is based on point-wise behaviors of maximal functions of u / d s u/d^{s} .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 35R09, 35B65

1.
Introduction

In this paper, we investigate the weak solution to the following nonlocal problem:

(1.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{L}_{A}u&=f&&\qquad\mbox{in $\Omega$},\\
u&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in ${\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus\Omega$},\end{aligned}\right.

where
\mathcal{L}_{A}
is the nonlocal operator defined as

(1.2)

\mathcal{L}_{A}u(x)=\mathrm{P.V.}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\frac{u(x)-u(y)}{|x-y|^{n+2s}}A(x,y)\,dy\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

where
n\geq 2
,
s\in(0,1)
and the associated kernel coefficient
A=A(x,y):{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is measurable and satisfies

(1.3)

A(x,y)=A(y,x)\quad\text{and}\quad\Lambda^{-1}\leq A(x,y)\leq\Lambda\quad\mbox{for any $x,y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}$}

for some constant
\Lambda\geq 1
, and
\Omega
is
C^{1,\alpha}
-domain for some
\alpha\in(0,s)
.

In the literature, operators as in (
1.2
) are known as the fractional Laplacian type nonlocal operator and they appear in ample amount of physical models, see for instance
[
FR24
,
DPV12
]
and references therein. Particularly, when the coefficient
A
is constant, (
1.2
) corresponds to the well-known standard fractional Laplacian. Heuristically, under a suitable structure assumption on the coefficient
A
, operator
\mathcal{L}u
can be understood as a nonlocal analog of the divergence form operator of the kind
\mathrm{div}(M(x)Du)
, where
M
satisfies the uniform ellipticity conditions (see for instance,
[
FR24
, Chapter 2]
).

Taking into account the aforementioned observations, it is customary to mention fine regularity results for local problems with different kinds of coefficients. To this end, let us consider the following problem:

(1.4)

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}(M(x)Du)=f,

where the coefficient function
M
is uniformly elliptic and bounded, and
f
is a measurable function.
It is by now classical that weak solutions of (
1.4
) are Hölder continuous and their gradients have a slightly better integrability than the natural energy space under some suitable integrability assumptions on
f
but without imposing any regularity assumption on the coefficient function
M
. Some available counter examples assert that this is the best regularity one can expect in this case. Subsequently, it was observed that under small BMO assumption on
M
, the gradient of the solution exhibits higher integrability properties in the spirit of the classical Calderón-Zygmund theory known for the Laplacian (cf.
[
CP98
,
BW04
]
).
It is also known that even the continuity of the coefficient
M
is not sufficient to conclude the boundedness of the gradient (see for instance,
[
JMV09
]
). Nevertheless, the classical theory suggests that the Dini continuity is sufficient to get such a bound on the gradient when
f
is in the Lorentz space
L^{n,1}
. The latter condition is also optimal in this regard (see for instance,
[
Cia92
,
DM10
]
). We also refer to
[
CM11
]
for global Lipschitz regularity results for the solution of the Dirichlet problem with the homogeneous boundary condition under a suitable regularity assumption on the domain.
Furthermore, concerning the Hölder continuity of the gradient of a solution
u
, we have that when
M\in C^{\alpha}
and
f\in L^{\frac{n}{1-\alpha},\infty}
, then
Du\in C^{\alpha}
(cf.
[
KM12
]
).

Concerning nonlocal problems, interior regularity for linear problems is now rather well understood. In particular, for Hölder’s regularity, Harnack’s inequality, self-improving properties and zero-order potential estimates, without being exhaustive, we refer to
[
Kas09
,
KW24
,
KMS15a
,
KMS15
,
KLL23
,
BDOR24
]
. For higher interior regularity results obtained under additional regularity assumptions on the kernel coefficient, we refer to
[
Now21
,
Now23a
,
Now23
,
BL17
,
BLS18
,
MSY21
,
Coz17
,
BDLBS24
,
BDLBS24a
,
DKLN24
,
CS11
,
Fal20
,
FR24a
,
KNS22
]
and references therein.

In contrast to local problems, the boundary regularity for nonlocal problems witnesses quite a peculiar behavior. Particularly, the order of interior Hölder regularity of solutions does not match that of up to the boundary irrespective of the smoothness assumption on the boundary and data of the problem (e.g., the coefficient and the right-hand side). For instance, it has been observed that the function
u(x):=(1-|x|^{2})^{s}_{+}
solves

\displaystyle(-\Delta)^{s}u={\mathrm{const.}}\quad\text{in }B_{1}^{+};\quad u=0\mbox{ \, \, in }{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{1}^{+},

which fails to be more regular than
C^{s}
up to the set
\{x\in B_{1}\,:\,x_{n}=0\}
. More precisely, in a recent article
[
RW24
]
, a global
C^{s}
regularity is established for the weak solution to (
1.1
) in
C^{1,\alpha}
domains when the kernel coefficient is merely translation invariant. Prior to this work, similar results were obtained only for kernels satisfying both the translation invariant condition and a homogeneity condition. Recently, in
[
Gru24
]
, the regularity assumption on the boundary is relaxed to that of
C^{1,\mathrm{Dini}}
to obtain such an optimal regularity up to the boundary when the right-hand side datum is bounded. In this direction, some related global regularity results concerning the Hölder continuity for solutions to nonlocal problems can be found in
[
FR24
,
KKP16
,
IMS16
,
GKS23
]
and references therein. We refer to
[
AFLY23
,
BN23
,
LPPS15
]
and their references for global Calderón-Zygmund type theory involving the solution
u
. In a recent preprint
[
KW24a
]
, the non-translation invariant kernel case is dealt with. In particular, it is proved that when the domain is of
C^{1,\alpha}
, the kernel coefficient
A
is in
C^{\alpha}
in the sense of (
5.73
) below and
f\in L^{q}
, where
q>n/s
, then weak solutions are in the space
C^{s}
up to the boundary of the domain.

In recent times, it has become quite an intriguing task to look for some higher-order boundary regularity theory involving the solutions that could improve with an improvement on the regularity of the data of the problem; that is, not as restrictive as the Hölder continuity or Sobolev regularity of solutions up to the boundary. In this direction, the regularity theory for the quotient
u/d^{s}
, where
d(x)\coloneqq\mathrm{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)
, has attracted much attention from researchers since the seminal works of Ros-Oton-Jerra
[
RS17
,
RS16
,
RS16a
,
RS14
]
and Grubb
[
Gru15
,
Gru18
]
. Heuristically, when
s\to 1^{-}
, the quotient
u/d^{s}
can be viewed as the normal derivative of
u
at the boundary points. In the nonlocal setting, it has been observed that the function
u/d^{s}
enjoys much higher regularity up to the boundary compared to that of only
u
, if the data of the problem is smooth enough. Specifically, in
[
RS14
]
, it is established that when
f\in L^{\infty}
and the domain is of
C^{1,1}
, then
u/d^{s}\in C^{\beta}
, for some small
\beta>0
. Subsequently, in
[
Gru15
]
, it is proved that when the kernel coefficient is translation invariant, smooth and homogeneous, the right-hand side is also smooth and the domain is
C^{\infty}
, then
u/d^{s}
is also smooth up to the boundary. In fact, in the same paper, regularity theory for linear pseudo-differential operators is established. Some higher Hölder continuity results of
u/d^{s}
are also obtained for translation invariant and homogeneous kernels in the works
[
Fal19
,
AR20
]
. We refer to the recent monograph
[
FR24
]
for a detailed discussion on similar results for problems with different kinds of homogeneous or translation invariant kernel coefficients. In
[
IM24
,
IMS20
]
, the Hölder continuity of
u/d^{s}
for nonlinear fractional p-Laplacian equations is proved. On the other hand, we refer to
[
CKR23
,
DR24
,
Gru15
,
Gru18
]
for various kinds of global Calderón-Zygmund type estimates of the function
u/d^{s}
for nonlocal problems with different types of kernel but not including non-translation invariant ones. Recently, in
[
KW24a
]
, for non-translation invariant and Hölder continuous kernel in the sense of (
5.73
), Hölder regularity of
u/d^{s}
is proved provided that the frozen kernel as in (
5.71
) is homogeneous.

To the best of our knowledge, there is no regularity result available for the quotient
u/d^{s}
in the spirit of Calderón-Zygmund for the solution of (
1.1
) with possibly discontinuous coefficient and non-translational invariant
A
. Moreover, in the limiting case, to get the boundedness of
u/d^{s}
, the available works in the literature seem to impose the condition of Hölder continuity (in some form) or the translation invariance property on the coefficient function.

Our aim is to find suitable regularity assumptions on the associated kernel coefficient
A
to derive sharp global Calderón-Zygmund type estimates for
u/d^{s}
with respect to the datum
f
. To be precise, we will prove the following:

(a)

If
A
is VMO in
B_{R}(x_{0})\times B_{R}(x_{0})
, then

f\in L^{q}(\Omega\cap B_{R}(x_{0}))\Longrightarrow u/d^{s}\in L^{\frac{nq}{n-qs}}(\Omega\cap B_{R/2}(x_{0}))

for any
q\in[2_{*},n/s)
, where
2_{*}\coloneqq\frac{2n}{n+2s}
.

(b)

If
A
is Dini continuous in
B_{R}(x_{0})\times B_{R}(x_{0})
, then

\displaystyle f\in L^{n/s,1}(\Omega\cap B_{R}(x_{0}))\Longrightarrow u/d^{s}\in{L^{\infty}(\Omega\cap B_{R/2}(x_{0}))}\quad\text{and}\quad u\in C^{s}(B_{R/2}(x_{0})).

(c)

If
A
is
\alpha
-Hölder continuous in
B_{R}(x_{0})\times B_{R}(x_{0})
, then

f\in L^{q}(\Omega\cap B_{R}(x_{0}))\Longrightarrow u/d^{s}\in W^{\sigma,\frac{nq}{n-(s-\sigma)q}}(\Omega\cap B_{R/2}(x_{0}))

and

f\in L^{\frac{n}{s-\sigma},\infty}(\Omega\cap B_{R}(x_{0}))\Longrightarrow u/d^{s}\in C^{\sigma}(\Omega\cap B_{R/2}(x_{0}))

for any
q\in[2_{*},\infty)
and
\sigma\in(0,\min\{\alpha,s\})
.

Before presenting a localized version of problem (
1.1
) and prescribing a notion of a weak solution to it, we first recall relevant function spaces.

Let us fix a domain
Q\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.
We first recall the fractional Sobolev space
W^{\sigma,p}(Q)
with
\sigma\in(0,1)
and
1\leq p<\infty
, which is defined as

W^{\sigma,p}(Q)\coloneqq\{g:Q\to{\mathbb{R}}\,:\,\|g\|_{W^{\sigma,p}(Q)}<\infty\},

where

\|g\|_{W^{\sigma,p}(Q)}\coloneqq\left(\int_{Q}\int_{Q}\frac{|g(x)-g(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{n+\sigma p}}\,dx\,dy\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}+\left(\int_{Q}|g|^{p}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

We also recall the following tail space

L^{1}_{2s}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\coloneqq\left\{g:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}\,:\,\|g\|_{L^{1}_{2s}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}\coloneqq\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\frac{|g(y)|}{(1+|y|)^{n+2s}}\,dy<\infty\right\},

and for any
B_{R}(x_{0})\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, we write

\mathrm{Tail}(g;B_{R}(x_{0}))\coloneqq R^{2s}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{R}(x_{0})}\frac{|g(y)|}{|y-x_{0}|^{n+2s}}\,dy,

which was first introduced in
[
DKP16
]
.
For any measurable function
h:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, we define

X_{h}^{s,2}(Q,Q^{\prime})\coloneqq\{g\in W^{s,2}(Q^{\prime})\,:g\equiv h\quad\text{in }{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus Q\},

where
Q^{\prime}
is a domain satisfying
Q\Subset Q^{\prime}
.
Since we note from
[
BLS18
, Lemma 2.11]
that
X_{0}^{s,2}(Q,Q^{\prime})\subset X_{0}^{s,2}(Q,{\mathbb{R}}^{n})
for any
Q^{\prime}\Supset Q
, we can write

X_{0}^{s,2}(Q)\coloneqq X_{0}^{s,2}(Q,{\mathbb{R}}^{n}).

We now introduce a localized problem of (
1.1
) and define its weak solutions.

Definition 1.1
.

Let us fix
f\in L^{2_{*}}(\Omega\cap B_{R}(x_{0}))
. We say that
u\in W^{s,2}(B_{R}(x_{0}))\cap L^{1}_{2s}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
is a weak solution to

(1.5)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{L}_{A}u&=f&&\qquad\mbox{in $\Omega\cap B_{R}(x_{0})$},\\
u&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $B_{R}(x_{0})\setminus\Omega$},\end{aligned}\right.

if
u\in W^{s,2}(B)
for some
B\Supset\Omega\cap B_{R}(x_{0})
and for any
\psi\in X^{s,2}_{0}(\Omega\cap B_{R}(x_{0}))
, there holds

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\frac{(u(x)-u(y))(\psi(x)-\psi(y))}{|x-y|^{n+2s}}A(x,y)\,dx\,dy=\int_{\Omega\cap B_{R}(x_{0})}f\psi\,dx.

Remark 1.2
.

Observe from
[
BLS18
, Proposition 2.12]
that there is a unique weak solution
u\in X^{s,2}_{0}(\Omega)
to (
1.1
) when
f\in L^{2_{*}}(\Omega)
. Therefore, the solution
u
to (
1.1
) is also a weak solution to (
1.5
).

Since the problem (
1.5
) is scaling-invariant, it suffices to confine our analysis to a solution
u\in W^{s,2}(B_{1})\cap L^{1}_{2s}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
to

(1.6)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{L}_{A}u&=f&&\qquad\mbox{in $\Omega\cap B_{1}$},\\
u&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $B_{1}\setminus\Omega$}.\end{aligned}\right.

We now introduce a notion of
\delta
-vanishing condition for the kernel coefficient
A
.

Definition 1.3
.

Let us fix
\delta
and
R>0
. We say that
A
is
(\delta,R)
-vanishing in
B\times B\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
if for any
B_{r}(x_{0})\subset B
with
r\in(0,R]
,

\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{B_{r}(x_{0})\times B_{r}(x_{0})}|A-(A)_{B_{r}(x_{0})\times B_{r}(x_{0})}|\,dx\,dy\leq\delta.

We now state the main results of the paper. We first present the
L^{q}
estimates of
u/d^{s}
when the coefficient is possibly discontinuous.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
u\in W^{s,2}(B_{1})\cap L^{1}_{2s}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
be a weak solution to (
1.6
). For any
q\in[2_{*},n/s)
and
\rho_{0}>0
, there is a constant
\delta=\delta(n,s,\Lambda,q)
such that if
A
is
(\delta,\rho_{0})
-vanishing in
B_{1}\times B_{1}
, then we have

(1.7)

\|u/d^{s}\|_{L^{\frac{nq}{n-sq}}(\Omega\cap B_{1/2})}\leq c\left(\|u\|_{L^{1}_{2s}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}+\|f\|_{L^{q}(\Omega\cap B_{1})}\right)

for some constant
c=c(n,s,\Lambda,\alpha,q,\rho_{0})
.

Remark 1.5
.

We assert that the estimates of Theorem
1.4
are sharp when we only require
A
to satisfy a
(\delta,R)
-vanishing condition. Indeed, in the Appendix
A
, for any
\varepsilon>0
, we construct a weak solution
u
to (
1.5
) with
A\equiv 1
and
f\in L^{q}(\Omega\cap B_{1})
such that
u/d^{s}\notin L^{\frac{nq}{n-sq}+\varepsilon}(\Omega\cap B_{1/2})
. We also point out that in the forthcoming Section
5.3
, we prove that the estimate (
1.7
) holds when
A
satisfies a slightly different assumption from Definition
1.3
. In particular, such an assumption holds for a translation invariance-type kernel coefficient.

Remark 1.6
.

In
[
AG23
]
, it is shown that
u/d^{s}\in H^{s,q}\subset L^{\frac{nq}{n-sq}}
when
A\equiv 1
or
A
is translation invariant and homogeneous, and
\Omega
is a
C^{1,\tau}
-domain, where
\tau>2s
. Thus, concerning a higher-integrability result of
u/d^{s}
, Theorem
1.4
relaxes the regularity assumption on the kernel coefficient
A
by allowing it to have some discontinuity and the domain to be
C^{1,\alpha}
-regular for any
\alpha>0
.

We introduce notions of Dini and Hölder continuity of the kernel coefficient.

Definition 1.7
.

We say that
A
is Dini continuous in
B_{R}(x_{0})\times B_{R}(x_{0})
if
there is a non-decreasing function
{{\omega}}:{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
with
{{\omega}}(0)=0
such that

|A(x_{1},y_{1})-A(x_{2},y_{2})|\leq{{\omega}}(\max\{|x_{1}-x_{2}|,|y_{1}-y_{2}|\})\quad\text{if }x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}\in B_{R}(x_{0})

and

(1.8)

\int_{0}^{R}{{{\omega}}(\rho)}\frac{\,d\rho}{\rho}<\infty.

In particular, if
\omega(\rho)\leq c\rho^{\alpha}
for some constant
c\geq 1
, then we say that
A
is Hölder continuous of order
\alpha
in
B_{R}(x_{0})
.

We now provide a boundedness result of
u/d^{s}
when
A
is Dini continuous.

Theorem 1.8
.

Let
u\in W^{s,2}(\Omega\cap B_{1})\cap L^{1}_{2s}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
be a weak solution to (
1.6
) with
A
being Dini continuous in
B_{1}\times B_{1}
. If
f\in L^{n/s,1}(\Omega\cap B_{1})
, then we have

\displaystyle\|u/d^{s}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega\cap B_{1/2})}\leq c\left(\|u\|_{L^{1}_{2s}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}+\|f\|_{L^{n/s,1}(\Omega\cap B_{1})}\right),

where
c=c(n,s,\Lambda,\alpha,{{\omega}})
.

From this, we deduce a global
C^{s}
-regularity.

Corollary 1.9
.

Let
u\in W^{s,2}(\Omega\cap B_{1})\cap L^{1}_{2s}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
be a weak solution to (
1.6
) with
A
being Dini continuous in
B_{1}\times B_{1}
. If
f\in L^{n/s,1}(\Omega\cap B_{1})
, then we have

\displaystyle[u]_{C^{s}(B_{1/2})}\leq c\left(\|u\|_{L^{1}_{2s}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}+\|f\|_{L^{n/s,1}(\Omega\cap B_{1})}\right),

where
c=c(n,s,\Lambda,\alpha,{{\omega}})
.

Remark 1.10
.

We would like to mention that our estimates given in Theorem
1.8
and Corollary
1.9
are true when
A
is Dini continuous in a weaker sense as in (
5.72
). Indeed, we employ a well-known result that a weak solution
v
to (
1.6
) with a constant kernel coefficient and
f=0
satisfies
v/\phi\in C^{\varepsilon}
for some
\varepsilon>0
and some barrier function
\phi
which is comparable to
d^{s}
, in order to obtain decay estimates as in Lemma
5.4
. However, such a Hölder regularity is also true for a weak solution
v
to (
1.6
) with a translation invariant kernel instead of constant kernels (see
[
RW24
, Theorem 1.6]
).
Thus, we obtain Theorem
1.8
and Corollary
1.9
which also hold for translation invariant kernel coefficients (see Section
5.3
for details).

Remark 1.11
.

Global
C^{s}
-regularity for solution to (
1.6
) is known only when
A
satisfies (
5.73
) below, and
f\in L^{n/s+\varepsilon}(\Omega\cap B_{1})
for any
\varepsilon>0
. However, our result as in Corollary
1.9
allows us to weaken the regularity assumptions on both the kernel coefficient and the right-hand side.

Theorem 1.12
.

Let
u\in W^{s,2}(\Omega\cap B_{1})\cap L^{1}_{2s}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
be a weak solution to (
1.6
) with
A
being Hölder continuous of order
\alpha
in
B_{1}\times B_{1}
.
Let us also assume that, there is a constant
K\geq 1
such that

(1.9)

[A]_{C^{\alpha}(B_{1}\times B_{1})}\leq K.

If
\sigma\in(0,\alpha)
and
f\in L^{q}(\Omega\cap B_{1})
with
q\in[2_{*},n/(s-\sigma))
, then

(1.10)

\|u/d^{s}\|_{W^{\sigma,\frac{nq}{n-(s-\sigma)q}}(\Omega\cap B_{1/2})}\leq c\left(\|u\|_{L^{1}_{2s}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}+\|f\|_{L^{q}(\Omega\cap B_{1})}\right),

where
c=c(n,s,\Lambda,\alpha,q,\sigma,K)
.

Remark 1.13
.

As in Remark
1.5
, we also observe that the estimates of Theorem
1.12
are sharp. Since if the estimates (
1.10
) were true when the space
W^{\sigma,\frac{nq}{n-(s-\sigma)q}}
is replaced by either
W^{\sigma+\varepsilon,\frac{nq}{n-(s-\sigma)q}}
or
u/d^{s}\in W^{\sigma,\frac{nq}{n-(s-\sigma)q}+\varepsilon}
for some
\varepsilon>0
, then by the Sobolev embedding, the estimates (
1.7
) would hold with
L^{\frac{nq}{n-sq}}
replaced by
L^{\frac{nq}{n-sq}+\varsigma}
for some small
\varsigma>0
. This is not possible by the sharpness result of Theorem
1.4
. Therefore, the estimates (
1.10
) are also sharp.

Theorem 1.14
.

Let
u\in W^{s,2}(\Omega\cap B_{1})\cap L^{1}_{2s}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
be a weak solution to (
1.6
), where
A
is Hölder continuous of order
\alpha
in
B_{1}\times B_{1}
and satisfies (
1.9
) for some constant
K\geq 1
. For any
\sigma\in(0,\alpha)
, if
f\in L^{n/(s-\sigma),\infty}(\Omega\cap B_{1})
, then

[u/d^{s}]_{C^{\sigma}(\overline{\Omega}\cap B_{1/2})}\leq c\left(\|u\|_{L^{1}_{2s}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}+\|f\|_{L^{n/(s-\sigma),\infty}(\Omega\cap B_{1})}\right),

where
c=c(n,s,\Lambda,\alpha,\sigma,K)
.

Remark 1.15
.

We remark that in Section
5.3
, we obtain the estimates given in Theorem
1.12
and Theorem
1.14
are true under the assumption that
A
satisfies slightly different continuity condition as in (
5.73
) below and the frozen kernel
A_{z}
which is defined in (
5.71
) below is homogeneous, see Section
5.3
for more details.

Remark 1.16
.

A few comments are in order concerning the regularity assumption on the right-hand side term
f
in Theorem
1.14
as compared to the available results. Let us fix
q\in(\frac{n}{s},\frac{n}{s-\alpha})
with
\sigma\coloneqq s-n/q
. We note from
[
KW24a
]
that

f\in L^{q}(\Omega\cap B_{1})\Longrightarrow u/d^{s}\in C^{s-n/q}(\overline{\Omega}\cap B_{1/2}).

However, our result implies

f\in L^{q,\infty}(\Omega\cap B_{1})\Longrightarrow u/d^{s}\in C^{s-n/q}(\overline{\Omega}\cap B_{1/2}).

Since

L^{q}(\Omega\cap B_{1})\subsetneq L^{q,\infty}(\Omega\cap B_{1})\subsetneq L^{q-\varepsilon}(\Omega\cap B_{1})

for any
\varepsilon>0
, Theorem
1.14
can be seen as a borderline result.

Let us give an overview of our approach to get the desired results. Indeed, we start with a localization argument which allows us to assume
u\in W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
and
u\equiv 0
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{2}
. In addition, if
A
is continuous in
B_{1}\times B_{1}
, we can always assume
A
is globally continuous by such an argument.

For the case when
A
is VMO, we employ an approximation method as in
[
CP98
]
, and Vitali’s type covering argument, in order to derive

\|M^{\Omega}_{2^{-6}}(u/d^{s})\|_{L^{\frac{nq}{n-sq}}}\lesssim\|u\|_{L^{1}_{2s}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}+\|M^{\Omega}_{2_{*}s,2^{-3}}(|f|^{2_{*}})\|_{L^{\frac{nq}{2_{*}(n-qs)}}}^{\frac{1}{2_{*}}}.

For the case when the associated kernel coefficient is continuous, we establish a suitable excess decay estimate as below:

\displaystyle E(u;B_{\rho r})

\displaystyle\lesssim\rho^{\alpha}E(u;B_{r})+\rho^{-n}({\boldsymbol{\omega}}(r)+{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{G}(r))(E(u;B_{r})+(u/d^{s})_{B_{r}})

\displaystyle\quad+\rho^{-n}\max\{d(0),r\}^{-s}\mathrm{Tail}(u(\cdot){\boldsymbol{\omega}}(|\cdot|);B_{r})+\rho^{-n}\left(\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{\Omega\cap B_{r}}(r^{s}|f|)^{2_{*}}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2_{*}}},

where

\displaystyle{E}(u;B_{\rho})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{B_{\rho}}|u/\phi-(u/\phi)_{B_{\rho}}|\,dx+\max\{\phi(0),\rho^{s}\}^{-1}\mathrm{Tail}(u-(u/\phi)_{B_{\rho}}\phi;B_{\rho})

and the function
\phi
is a barrier function that is comparable to the distance function
d^{s}
(see Section
5
for more details about this barrier) and we have written
{\boldsymbol{\omega}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{G}
as a local and a global oscillation-type quantity of the coefficient
A
(see (
5.9
) below), respectively. We would like to mention that this excess functional was first introduced by
[
KW24a
]
. In light of the localization argument, we can handle the term
{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{G}(\cdot)
as in the local term
{\boldsymbol{\omega}}(\cdot)
. Therefore, based on the approach given in
[
DM10a
]
and splitting the tail term appropriately together with suitable use of a weight function
{\boldsymbol{\omega}}(|\cdot|)
, we finally establish

(1.11)

\displaystyle M^{\Omega}_{2^{-6}}(u/d^{s})(0)\lesssim\|u\|_{L^{1}_{2s}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}+W^{|f|^{2_{*}}\mbox{\Large$\chi$}_{\Omega}}_{\frac{2_{*}s}{2_{*}s+1},2_{*}+1}(0,2^{-2}),

if
A
is Dini continuous.

Based on arguments given in
[
KM12
]
together with (
1.11
), we now obtain a more sharp estimate

\displaystyle M^{\#,\Omega}_{\sigma,2^{-6}}(u/d^{s})(0)\lesssim\|u\|_{L^{1}_{2s}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}+W^{|f|^{2_{*}}\mbox{\Large$\chi$}_{\Omega}}_{\frac{2_{*}s}{2_{*}s+1},2_{*}+1}(0,2^{-2})+\left({M}^{\Omega}_{2_{*}(s-\sigma),2^{-2}}(|f|^{2_{*}})(0)\right)^{\frac{1}{2_{*}}}

if
A
is Hölder continuous with order
\alpha
, where
\sigma\in(0,\alpha)
.
We point out that in the proof, we indeed use a nonlocal fractional sharp maximal function which was first introduced by
[
DN23
]
(see (
5.39
) for its precise definition).

The rest of the article has the following organization. In Section 2, we introduce some notations and provide embedding results. The localization argument and some well-known regularity results of solutions to fractional Laplacian type equations are also mentioned in this section. In Section 3, we provide comparison estimates. In Section 4, we prove Theorem
1.8
. In Section 5, we prove Theorem
1.8
, Corollary
1.9
, Theorem
1.12
and Theorem
1.14
. In the appendix, we provide examples to show the sharpness of our estimates.

2.
Preliminaries

Let us denote by
c
to mean a universal constant which is bigger or equal to 1. In addition, a parenthesis is used to denote the relevant dependencies of the constant
c
, e.g.,
c=c(n,s)
, if the constant
c
depends only on
n
and
s
.

We first observe that there are constants
\rho_{0}=\rho_{0}(\Omega)\in(0,1]
and
c_{0}=c_{0}(\Omega)\in(0,1]
such that for any
\rho\in(0,\rho_{0}]
,

\inf_{y_{0}\in\partial\Omega}\frac{|B_{\rho}(y_{0})\setminus\Omega|}{|B_{\rho}|}\geq c_{0}\quad\text{and}\quad\inf_{x_{0}\in\overline{\Omega}}\frac{|B_{\rho}(x_{0})\cap\Omega|}{|B_{\rho}|}\geq c_{0},

as
\Omega
is a
C^{1,\alpha}
-domain. Therefore, we deduce that for any
\rho\in(0,1]
, there hold

(2.1)

\inf_{y_{0}\in\partial\Omega}\frac{|B_{\rho}(y_{0})\setminus\Omega|}{|B_{\rho}|}\geq c_{1}\quad\text{and}\quad\inf_{x_{0}\in\overline{\Omega}}\frac{|B_{\rho}(x_{0})\cap\Omega|}{|B_{\rho}|}\geq c_{1},

where
c_{1}=c_{0}\rho_{0}^{n}
depends only on
\Omega
.

For convenience in writing, we abbreviate

\mathsf{data}=\mathsf{data}(n,s,\Lambda,\alpha,\Omega).

We now recall the truncated Wolff potential of a function
g\in L^{1}(B_{2R}(x_{0}))
as

\mathbf{W}^{|g|}_{\beta,p}(z,r)\coloneqq\int_{0}^{r}\left(\frac{|g|(B_{\rho}(z))}{\rho^{n-\beta p}}\right)^{\frac{1}{p-1}}\frac{\,d\rho}{\rho}\quad\text{for any }r\in(0,R]\quad\text{and}\quad z\in B_{R}(x_{0}),

where
p\geq 1
and
\beta>0
.
By
[
Cia11
]
, we have

(2.2)

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{W}^{|g|}_{\beta,p}(z,R)\|_{L^{\frac{nq(p-1)}{n-\beta pq}}(B_{R/2}(x_{0}))}\leq c\|g\|_{L^{q}(B_{2R}(x_{0}))}

for some constant
c=c(n,q,p,\beta,R)
whenever
\beta p<q
. In addition, we also observe from
[
Cia11
]
that

(2.3)

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{W}^{|g|}_{\beta,p}(z,R)\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R/2}(x_{0}))}\leq c\|g\|_{L^{\frac{n}{\beta p},\frac{1}{(p-1)}}(B_{2R}(x_{0}))}

for some constant
c=c(n,p,\beta)
, whenever
\beta p<n
. We refer to
[
SW71
]
for more details about the Lorentz spaces.

In what follows, when we consider the set
\Omega\cap B_{R}(x_{0})
, we always assume
x_{0}\in\overline{\Omega}
.

For any
g\in L^{1}(\Omega\cap B_{2R}(x_{0}))
with
R\leq 1
and
\beta\in[0,n)
, we define a fractional maximal function by

M^{\Omega}_{\beta,R}(g)(z)\coloneqq\sup_{0<\rho<R}\rho^{\beta}\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{\Omega\cap B_{\rho}(z)}|g|\,dx\quad\text{for any }z\in\overline{\Omega}\cap B_{R}(x_{0}).

In particular, we write
M^{\Omega}_{R}(g)(z)\coloneqq M^{\Omega}_{0,R}(g)(z)
.
By (
2.1
), we have for any
z\in\overline{\Omega}\cap B_{R}(x_{0})
,

M^{\Omega}_{\beta,R}(g)(z)\leq c\sup_{0<\rho<R}\rho^{\beta}\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{B_{\rho}(z)}|g\mbox{\Large$\chi$}_{\Omega}|\,dx\leq cM_{\beta,R}(g\mbox{\Large$\chi$}_{\Omega})(z)

for some constant
c=c(n,\Omega)
, where
M_{\beta,R}(g)
is the standard truncated fractional maximal function. Therefore, using this and
[
KS03
, Theorem 3.1]
, we have

(2.4)

\displaystyle\|M^{\Omega}_{\beta,R}(g)(z)\|_{L^{\frac{nq}{n-q\beta}}(\Omega\cap B_{R}(x_{0}))}\leq c\|M_{\beta,R}(g\mbox{\Large$\chi$}_{\Omega})(z)\|_{L^{\frac{nq}{n-q\beta}}(\Omega\cap B_{R}(x_{0}))}

\displaystyle\leq c\|g\mbox{\Large$\chi$}_{\Omega}\|_{L^{q}(B_{2R}(x_{0}))}

\displaystyle\leq c\|g\|_{L^{q}(\Omega\cap B_{2R}(x_{0}))}

for some constant
c=c(n,\beta,R,\Omega)
. In addition, from
[
DN23
, Proposition 2.5]
, we get

(2.5)

\displaystyle\|M^{\Omega}_{\beta,R}(g)(z)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega\cap B_{R}(x_{0}))}\leq\|M_{\beta,R}(g\mbox{\Large$\chi$}_{\Omega})(z)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega\cap B_{R}(x_{0}))}

\displaystyle\leq c\|g\mbox{\Large$\chi$}_{\Omega}\|_{L^{n/\beta,\infty}(B_{2R}(x_{0}))}

\displaystyle\leq c\|g\|_{L^{n/\beta,\infty}(\Omega\cap B_{2R}(x_{0}))}

for some constant
c=c(n,\beta,R,\Omega)
. We refer to
[
SW71
]
for more details about the Marcinkiewicz spaces.
We next introduce a fractional sharp maximal function as

M^{{\#,\Omega}}_{\beta,R}(g)(z)\coloneqq\sup_{0<\rho\leq R}\rho^{-\beta}\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{\Omega\cap B_{\rho}(z)}|g-(g)_{\Omega\cap B_{\rho}(z)}|\,dx\quad\text{for any }z\in\overline{\Omega}\cap B_{R}(x_{0}),

where
g\in L^{1}(\Omega\cap B_{2R}(x_{0}))
with
R\leq 1
and
\beta\in[0,1]
.

We first assert the following inequality.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
g\in L^{1}(\Omega\cap B_{2R}(x_{0}))
with
R\leq 1
. Then for any
\beta\in(0,1]
and all
x,y\in\overline{\Omega}\cap B_{R/4}(x_{0})
, we have

|g(x)-g(y)|\leq c\left(M^{{\#},\Omega}_{\beta,R}(g)(x)+M^{{\#},\Omega}_{\beta,R}(g)(y)\right)|x-y|^{\beta}

for some constant
c=c(n,\beta,\Omega)
.

Proof.

We will follow the same approach as in
[
KM14
, Proposition 1]
. Let us denote
\rho\coloneqq|x-y|\leq R/2
. Then we have

(2.6)

\Omega\cap B_{\rho}(y)\subset\Omega\cap B_{2\rho}(x).

By (
2.1
), we observe that

\displaystyle|(g)_{\Omega\cap B_{\rho_{i}}(x)}-(g)_{\Omega\cap B_{\rho_{i+1}}(x)}|\leq\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{\Omega\cap B_{\rho_{i+1}}(x)}|g-(g)_{\Omega\cap B_{\rho_{i}}(x)}|\,d\tilde{x}

\displaystyle\leq c\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{\Omega\cap B_{\rho_{i}}(x)}|g-(g)_{\Omega\cap B_{\rho_{i}}(x)}|\,d\tilde{x}

\displaystyle\leq c(2^{-i}\rho)^{\beta}M^{{\#},\Omega}_{\beta,R}(g)(x)

for some constant
c=c(n,\Omega)
, where
\rho_{i}=2^{-i}\rho
. Thus we have

\displaystyle|(g)_{\Omega\cap B_{\rho_{i}}(x)}-(g)_{\Omega\cap B_{\rho}(x)}|\leq c\rho^{\beta}M^{{\#},\Omega}_{\beta,R}(g)(x)

for some constant
c=c(n,\beta)
, which is independent of
i
. Using this and the fact that
\Omega\cap B_{\rho_{i}}(x)=B_{\rho_{i}}(x)
if
i
is sufficiently large, we have

(2.7)

\displaystyle|g(x)-(g)_{\Omega\cap B_{2\rho}(x)}|\leq c|x-y|^{\beta}M^{{\#},\Omega}_{\beta,R}(g)(x)

for some constant
c=c(n,\beta,\Omega)
. Similarly, we have

(2.8)

\displaystyle|g(y)-(g)_{\Omega\cap B_{\rho}(y)}|\leq c|x-y|^{\beta}M^{{\#},\Omega}_{\beta,R}(g)(y).

In addition, we get

\displaystyle|(g)_{\Omega\cap B_{2\rho}(x)}-(g)_{\Omega\cap B_{\rho}(y)}|\leq\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{\Omega\cap B_{\rho}(y)}|g-(g)_{\Omega\cap B_{2\rho}(x)}|\leq\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{\Omega\cap B_{2\rho}(x)}|g-(g)_{\Omega\cap B_{2\rho}(x)}|,

where we have used (
2.6
) and (
2.1
) for the last inequality.
Therefore, we obtain

(2.9)

\displaystyle|g(x)-g(y)|

\displaystyle\leq|g(x)-(g)_{\Omega\cap B_{2\rho}(x)}|+|g(y)-(g)_{\Omega\cap B_{\rho}(y)}|+|(g)_{\Omega\cap B_{2\rho}(x)}-(g)_{\Omega\cap B_{\rho}(y)}|.

Combining all the estimates (
2.7
), (
2.8
) and (
2.9
), we get the desired result of the lemma.
∎

Using this, we have the following fractional Sobolev embedding via the sharp maximal function.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
\beta\in(0,1)
and
p>1
. If
t\in(0,\beta)
and
g\equiv 0
on
B_{R}(x_{0})\setminus\Omega
with
R\leq 1
, then we have

\displaystyle\|g\|_{W^{t,q}(\Omega\cap B_{R/4}(x_{0}))}\leq c\|M^{\#,\Omega}_{\beta,R/4}(g)\|_{L^{p}(\Omega\cap B_{R/2}(x_{0}))}+c\|g\|_{L^{p}(\Omega\cap B_{R/2}(x_{0}))},

where
q\coloneqq\frac{np}{n-(\beta-t)p}
and
c=c(n,\beta,t,p,\Omega,R)
.

Proof.

We may assume
x_{0}=0
and
R=1
. We note that there is a radius
r_{0}=r_{0}(\Omega)\in(0,1/2]
such that for any
z\in\partial\Omega
, there is a
C^{1,\alpha}
function
\gamma\equiv\gamma_{z}:{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that

(2.10)

\Psi(\Omega\cap B_{r_{0}}(z))=\{(x^{\prime},x_{n})\in B_{r_{0}}(z)\,:x_{n}>\gamma(x^{\prime})\}\quad\text{and}\quad\|\gamma\|_{C^{1,\alpha}}\leq c

for some constant
c=c(\Omega)
, where
\Psi\equiv\Psi_{z}:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is a rotation around the point
z
. Let us write

\varPhi(x^{\prime},x_{n})\coloneqq(x^{\prime},x_{n}-\gamma(x^{\prime}))\quad\text{for any }x\in B_{r_{0}}(z).

Then there are constants
a_{0}=a_{0}(\Omega)\in(0,16)
and
r_{1}=r_{1}(\Omega)\in(0,1)
such that

(2.11)

(\varPhi\circ\Psi)(\Omega\cap B_{a_{0}r_{0}}(z))\subset B_{r_{1}}^{+}(z^{\prime},0)\subset B_{2r_{1}}^{+}(z^{\prime},0)\subset(\varPhi\circ\Psi)(\Omega\cap B_{r_{0}/8}(z))

and

(\varPhi\circ\Psi)(\partial\Omega\cap B_{a_{0}r_{0}}(z))\subset\{(x^{\prime},x_{n})\in B_{2r_{1}}\,:\,x_{n}=0\}\subset(\varPhi\circ\Psi)(\partial\Omega\cap B_{r_{0}/8}(z)),

where we write

B_{2r_{1}}^{+}(z^{\prime},0)\coloneqq\{(x^{\prime},x_{n})\in B_{2r_{1}}(z^{\prime},0)\,:\,x_{n}>0\}.

We first prove for any
z\in\partial\Omega
,

(2.12)

\|g\|_{W^{t,q}(\Omega\cap B_{a_{0}r_{0}}(z))}\leq c\|M^{\#,\Omega}_{\beta,1/4}(g)\|_{L^{p}(\Omega\cap B_{r_{0}}(z))}+c\|g\|_{L^{p}(\Omega\cap B_{r_{0}}(z))},

where
c=c(n,\beta,t,p,\Omega)
.
We now define

G(x)\coloneqq g((\varPhi\circ\Psi)^{-1}(x))

to observe that

(2.13)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega\cap B_{a_{0}r_{0}}(z)}\int_{\Omega\cap B_{a_{0}r_{0}}(z)}\frac{|g(x)-g(y)|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+tq}}\,dx\,dy

\displaystyle=\int_{(\varPhi\circ\Psi)(\Omega\cap B_{a_{0}r_{0}}(z))}\int_{(\varPhi\circ\Psi)(\Omega\cap B_{a_{0}r_{0}}(z))}\frac{|G(x)-G(y)|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+tq}}\frac{|x-y|^{n+tq}}{|(\varPhi\circ\Psi)^{-1}(x)-(\varPhi\circ\Psi)^{-1}(y)|^{n+tq}}\,dx\,dy

\displaystyle\leq\int_{B_{r_{1}}^{+}(z^{\prime},0)}\int_{B_{r_{1}}^{+}(z^{\prime},0)}\frac{|G(x)-G(y)|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+tq}}\,dx\,dy

for some constant
c=c(n,q,t,\Omega)
, where we have used change of variables, (
2.10
), (
2.11
) and

|\mathrm{det}(\nabla(\varPhi\circ\Psi)^{-1})|(x)=1.

We next define the function
\overline{G}:B_{2r_{1}}(z^{\prime},0)\to{\mathbb{R}}
as

\displaystyle\overline{G}(z^{\prime},z_{n})\coloneqq\begin{cases}G(z^{\prime},z_{n})&\quad\text{if }z_{n}\geq 0,\\
G(z^{\prime},-z_{n})&\quad\text{if }z_{n}<0.\end{cases}

We then observe that for any
x,y\in B_{2r_{1}}^{+}(z^{\prime},0)
,

(2.14)

\displaystyle|\overline{G}(x^{\prime},x_{n})-\overline{G}(y^{\prime},y_{n})|

\displaystyle=|g((\varPhi\circ\Psi)^{-1}(x))-g((\varPhi\circ\Psi)^{-1}(y))|

\displaystyle\leq c\left(M^{{\#},\Omega}_{\beta,r_{0}/2}(g)((\varPhi\circ\Psi)^{-1}(x))-M^{{\#},\Omega}_{\beta,r_{0}/2}(g)((\varPhi\circ\Psi)^{-1}(y))\right)|(\varPhi\circ\Psi)^{-1}(x)-(\varPhi\circ\Psi)^{-1}(y)|^{\beta}

\displaystyle\leq c\left(M^{{\#},\Omega}_{\beta,r_{0}/2}(g)((\varPhi\circ\Psi)^{-1}(x))-M^{{\#},\Omega}_{\beta,r_{0}/2}(g)((\varPhi\circ\Psi)^{-1}(y))\right)|x-y|^{\beta}

for some constant
c=c(n,\beta,\Omega)
, where we have used Lemma
2.1
and the second inequality in (
2.10
). Similarly, if
x,y\in B_{2r_{1}}(z^{\prime},0)
with
x_{n}<0
and
y_{n}>0
, then we get

(2.15)

\displaystyle|\overline{G}(x^{\prime},x_{n})-\overline{G}(y^{\prime},y_{n})|

\displaystyle=|{G}(x^{\prime},-x_{n})-{G}(y^{\prime},y_{n})|

\displaystyle=|g((\varPhi\circ\Psi)^{-1}(x^{\prime},-x_{n}))-g((\varPhi\circ\Psi)^{-1}(y))|

\displaystyle\leq c\left(M^{{\#},\Omega}_{\beta,r_{0}/2}(g((\varPhi\circ\Psi)^{-1})(x^{\prime},-x_{n})-M^{{\#},\Omega}_{\beta,r_{0}/2}(g)((\varPhi\circ\Psi)^{-1}(y))\right)|x-y|^{\beta},

where we have also used

|(x^{\prime},-x_{n})-(y^{\prime},y_{n})|\leq|x-y|\quad\text{if }x_{n}<0\quad\text{and}\quad y_{n}>0.

Combining (
2.14
) and (
2.15
), and noting (
2.10
), we deduce that

(2.16)

\displaystyle\sup_{0<|h|<r_{1}/8}\left(\int_{B_{r_{1}}(z^{\prime},0)}\frac{|\overline{G}(x+h)-\overline{G}(x)|^{p}}{|h|^{tp}}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{{p}}}

\displaystyle\leq c\left(\int_{B^{+}_{2r_{1}}(z^{\prime},0)}|M^{{\#},\Omega}_{\beta,r_{0}/2}(g)((\varPhi\circ\Psi)^{-1}(x))|^{{p}}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{{p}}}

\displaystyle\leq c\left(\int_{\Omega\cap B_{r_{0}/8}(z)}|M^{{\#},\Omega}_{\beta,1/4}(g)(x)|^{{p}}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{{p}}}.

As in the proof of
[
DN23
, Proposition 2.8]
, we get

(2.17)

\displaystyle\|\overline{G}\|_{W^{t,q}(B_{r_{1}}(z^{\prime},0))}\leq c\|\overline{G}\|^{q}_{L^{p}(B_{r_{1}}(z^{\prime},0))}+c\sup_{0<|h|<r_{1}/8}\left(\int_{(B_{r_{1}}(z^{\prime},0))}\frac{|\overline{G}(x+h)-\overline{G}(x)|^{{p}}}{|h|^{t{p}}}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{{p}}}

for some constant
c=c(n,s,\beta,p,\Omega)
.
Combining all the estimates (
2.13
), (
2.16
) and (
2.17
), we have (
2.12
).

On the other hand, if
B_{r_{0}}(z)\subset\Omega
, we get (
2.12
) directly from
[
DN23
, Proposition 2.8]
. Therefore, by a standard covering argument, we have the desired estimate of the lemma.
∎

We now present a localization argument that gives us the freedom to replace the local regularity assumptions on the weak solution and the kernel coefficient with the respective global ones.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
u\in W^{s,2}(B_{4R}(x_{0}))\cap L^{1}_{2s}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
be a weak solution to

(2.18)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{L}_{A}u&=f&&\qquad\mbox{in $\Omega\cap B_{R}(x_{0})$},\\
u&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $B_{R}(x_{0})\setminus\Omega$},\end{aligned}\right.

where
A
satisfies (
1.3
). For any kernel coefficient
a=a(x,y)
satisfying (
1.3
), there is a weak solution
\widetilde{u}\in W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
to

(2.19)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{L}_{\widetilde{A}}\widetilde{u}&=f+g_{1}+g_{2}&&\qquad\mbox{in $\Omega\cap B_{R/2}(x_{0})$},\\
\widetilde{u}&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $B_{R/2}(x_{0})\setminus\Omega$},\\
\widetilde{u}&=u&&\qquad\mbox{in $B_{5R/8}(x_{0})$},\end{aligned}\right.

where
\widetilde{A}
satisfies (
1.3
) along with
\widetilde{A}(x,y)=A(x,y)
in
B_{5R/8}(x_{0})\times B_{5R/8}(x_{0})
,
\widetilde{A}(x,y)=a(x,y)
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}\setminus(B_{3R/4}(x_{0})\times B_{3R/4}(x_{0}))
,
|g_{1}(x)|\leq cR^{-2s}|u(x)|
in
B_{R/2}(x_{0})
and

g_{2}\in L^{\infty}(B_{R/2}(x_{0}))
with the estimate

\|g_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R/2}(x_{0}))}\leq cR^{-2s}\mathrm{Tail}(u;B_{5R/8}(x_{0})).

Furthermore, if
A
is continuous in
B_{R}(x_{0})\times B_{R}(x_{0})
with

(2.20)

|A(x,y)-A(x_{0},x_{0})|\leq\omega(\max\{|x-x_{0}|,|y-x_{0}|\})\quad\text{for any }x,y\in B_{R}(x_{0}),

for some non-decreasing function
\omega:{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

with
\omega(0)=0
and if we select
a=A(x_{0},x_{0})
, then
\widetilde{A}
is continuous in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and satisfies

(2.21)

|\widetilde{A}(x,y)-\widetilde{A}(x_{0},x_{0})|\leq\widetilde{\omega}(\max\{|x-x_{0}|,|y-x_{0}|\})\quad\text{for any }x,y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

where

\displaystyle\widetilde{\omega}(\rho)=\begin{cases}\omega(\rho)\quad&\text{if }\rho\leq R,\\
0&\text{if }\rho>R.\end{cases}

Proof.

We may assume
x_{0}=0
. Let us take a cut off function
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(B_{3R/4})
with
\varphi\equiv 1
on
B_{5R/8}
. We denote

(2.22)

\widetilde{u}(x)\coloneqq u(x)\varphi(x)\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{A}(x,y)\coloneqq A(x,y)\varphi(x)\varphi(y)+a(x,y)(1-\varphi(x)\varphi(y)).

For any
\psi\in X_{0}^{s,2}(\Omega\cap B_{R/2})
, we have

(2.23)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\frac{(u(x)-u(y))(\psi(x)-\psi(y))}{|x-y|^{n+2s}}A(x,y)\,dx\,dy=\int_{\Omega\cap B_{R/2}}f\psi\,dx.

We next observe

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\frac{(\widetilde{u}(x)-\widetilde{u}(y))(\psi(x)-\psi(y))}{|x-y|^{n+2s}}(\widetilde{A}(x,y)-A(x,y))\,dx\,dy

\displaystyle=2\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{5R/8}}\int_{B_{R/2}}\frac{(u(x)-\widetilde{u}(y))\psi(x)}{|x-y|^{n+2s}}(\widetilde{A}(x,y)-A(x,y))\,dx\,dy

and

(2.24)

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\frac{[(\widetilde{u}(x)-\widetilde{u}(y))-({u}(x)-{u}(y))](\psi(x)-\psi(y))}{|x-y|^{n+2s}}A(x,y)\,dx\,dy

\displaystyle=2\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{5R/8}}\int_{B_{R/2}}\frac{(-\widetilde{u}(y)+{u}(y))\psi(x)}{|x-y|^{n+2s}}A(x,y)\,dx\,dy.

Combining the above three equations, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\frac{(\widetilde{u}(x)-\widetilde{u}(y))(\psi(x)-\psi(y))}{|x-y|^{n+2s}}\widetilde{A}(x,y)\,dx\,dy=\int_{\Omega\cap B_{R/2}}(f+g_{1}+g_{2})\psi,

where

g_{1}(x)=2\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{5R/8}}\frac{u(x)}{|x-y|^{n+2s}}(\widetilde{A}(x,y)-A(x,y))\,dy

and

g_{2}(x)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{5R/8}}\frac{(-4\widetilde{u}(y)+2{u}(y))}{|x-y|^{n+2s}}A(x,y)\,dy

for any
x\in B_{R/2}
. This implies that
\widetilde{u}\in W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
is a weak solution to (
2.18
) and

\displaystyle|g_{1}(x)|\leq cR^{-2s}|u(x)|\text{ for any }x\in B_{R/2}\quad\text{and}\quad\sup_{x\in B_{R/2}}|g_{2}(x)|\leq cR^{-2s}\mathrm{Tail}(u;B_{5R/8})

for some constant
c=c(n,s,\Lambda)
.

We now assume that
A
is continuous in
B_{R}\times B_{R}
satisfying (
2.20
) with
x_{0}=0
. Then

\displaystyle|\widetilde{A}(x,y)-\widetilde{A}(0,0)|

\displaystyle=|A(x,y)\varphi(x)\varphi(y)+A(0,0)(1-\varphi(x)\varphi(y))-A(0,0)|

\displaystyle=|(A(x,y)-A(0,0))\varphi(x)\varphi(y)|.

If
(x,y)\in B_{R}\times B_{R}
, we use (
2.20
) with
x_{0}=0
to get (
2.21
). On the other hand, if
(x,y)\notin{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}\setminus(B_{R}\times B_{R})
, we use the fact that
\varphi\equiv 0
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{3R/4}
to get (
2.21
). This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 2.4
.

We remark that if we do not want to localize the coefficient function
A
, then choosing a cut off function
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(B_{{15R/16}}(x_{0}))
with
\varphi\equiv 1
on
B_{7R/8}(x_{0})
taking into account (
2.23
) and (
2.24
), we observe that
\widetilde{u}\in W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
is a weak solution to

\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{L}_{{A}}\widetilde{u}&=f+g_{2}&&\qquad\mbox{in $\Omega\cap B_{3R/4}(x_{0})$},\\
\widetilde{u}&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $B_{3R/4}(x_{0})\setminus\Omega$},\\
\widetilde{u}&=u&&\qquad\mbox{in $B_{15R/16}(x_{0})$},\end{aligned}\right.

where
g_{2}\in L^{\infty}(B_{3R/4}(x_{0})
with

\|g_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{3R/4})(x_{0})}\leq cR^{-2s}\mathrm{Tail}(u;B_{15R/16}(x_{0}))

for some constant
=c(n,s,\Lambda)
.

We next provide the following fractional Hardy type inequality, which will be frequently used in the remaining parts.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
g\in W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
with
g=0
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus(\Omega\cap B_{R}(x_{0}))
with
R\leq 1
. Then we have

\int_{\Omega\cap B_{R}(x_{0})}\left|\frac{g}{d^{s}}\right|^{2}\,dx\leq\frac{c}{\max\left\{1,d^{s}(x_{0})/R^{s}\right\}^{2}}[g]^{2}_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}

for some constant
c=c(n,s,\Omega)
.

Proof.

We first assume
d(x_{0})>2R
. Then
B_{3R/2}(x_{0})\subset\Omega
and
d(x)>d(x_{0})/4
for any
x\in B_{5R/4}(x_{0})
. Therefore, by
[
Coz17
, Lemma 4.7]
, we have

(2.25)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega\cap B_{R}(x_{0})}\left|\frac{g}{d^{s}}\right|^{2}\,dx\leq c\frac{R^{2s}}{d^{2s}(x_{0})}\int_{\Omega\cap B_{R}(x_{0})}\left|\frac{g}{R^{s}}\right|^{2}\,dx\leq c\frac{R^{2s}}{d^{2s}(x_{0})}[g]^{2}_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}.

We next assume
d(x_{0})\leq 2R
. Then by
[
KW24a
, Lemma 2.4]
, we have

(2.26)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega\cap B_{R}(x_{0})}\left|\frac{g}{d^{s}}\right|^{2}\,dx\leq c[g]^{2}_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}

for some constant
c=c(n,s,\Omega)
. Combining two estimates (
2.25
) and (
2.26
) yields the desired result of the lemma.
∎

We prove self-improving properties of the fractional Laplacian type equations up to the boundary.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
R\in(0,1]
and let
w\in W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
be a weak solution to

(2.27)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{L}_{A}w&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\Omega\cap B_{4R}(x_{0})$},\\
w&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $B_{4R}(x_{0})\setminus\Omega$}.\end{aligned}\right.

Then there is a constant
\delta_{0}=\delta_{0}(\mathsf{data})\in(0,1)
such that
w\in W^{s+\frac{n\delta_{0}}{2+2\delta_{0}},2+2\delta_{0}}(B_{2R}(x_{0}))
with the estimate

(2.28)

R^{s+\frac{n\delta_{0}}{2+2\delta_{0}}-\frac{n}{2}}[w]_{W^{s+\frac{n\delta_{0}}{2+2\delta_{0}},2+2\delta_{0}}(B_{2R}(x_{0}))}\leq c\left(R^{s-\frac{n}{2}}\|w\|_{L^{2}(B_{3R}(x_{0}))}+\mathrm{Tail}(w;B_{3R}(x_{0}))\right),

where
c=c(n,s,\Lambda)
.

Proof.

We may assume
x_{0}=0
. For
\tau\coloneqq\min\{s,1-s\}/2
, we define

W(x,y)\coloneqq\frac{{w}(x)-{w}(y)}{|x-y|^{\tau}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mu(E)\coloneqq\int_{E}\frac{\,dx\,dy}{|x-y|^{n-2\tau}},

where
x,y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and
E\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}
is any measurable set. We are now going to prove that there is a constant
c=c(\mathsf{data})
such that

(2.29)

\displaystyle\left(\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\rho}(z)}W^{2}\,d\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{\sigma}\left(\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{\mathcal{B}_{2\rho}(z)}W^{q}\,d\mu_{\tau}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}+{\sigma}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{-k(s-\tau)}\left(\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{\mathcal{B}_{2^{k}\rho}}W^{q}\,d\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{q}},

whenever
\mathcal{B}_{\rho}(z)\coloneqq B_{\rho}(z)\times B_{\rho}(z)
for any
z\in B_{2R}
,
\rho\leq R/100
and
\sigma\in(0,1)
, where we have set

(2.30)

q\coloneqq\frac{2n+4\tau}{n+2s+2\tau}\quad\text{and}\quad\eta\coloneqq\frac{s-\tau}{n+2\tau}.

We first assume
B_{3\rho/2}(z)\cap\partial\Omega=\emptyset
. Then we have
({w}-({w})_{B_{\rho}(z)})\varphi^{2}\in X_{0}^{s,2}(\Omega\cap B_{4R})
for any
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(B_{5\rho/4}(z))
with
\varphi\equiv 1
on
B_{\rho}(z)
. Thus by testing
({w}-({w})_{B_{\rho}(z)})\varphi^{2}
to (
2.27
) with
x_{0}=0
and by following the same lines as in the proof of
[
KMS15a
, Theorem 3.2]
, we obtain

\displaystyle\rho^{-n+2s}[{w}]^{2}_{W^{s,2}(B_{\rho}(z))}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{\sigma}\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{B_{2\rho}(z)}|{w}-({w})_{B_{2\rho}(z)}|^{2}\,dx+\sigma\left(\rho^{2s}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{2\rho}(z)}\frac{|{w}-({w})_{B_{2\rho}(z)}|}{|y-z|^{n+2s}}\,dy\right)^{2}

for some constant
c=c(\mathsf{data})
. Thus, in this case, the proof of (
2.29
) can be completed exactly as in
[
KMS15a
, Proposition 4.4]
.

We next assume
B_{3\rho/2}(z)\cap\partial\Omega\neq\emptyset
. By (
2.1
), we have that for any
a\geq 2
with
a\rho\leq 1
,

|B_{a\rho}(z)\setminus\Omega|>c|B_{a\rho}|

holds for some constant
c=c(n,\Omega)
. If
1<a\rho\leq 4\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)
, then

|B_{a\rho}(z)\setminus\Omega|>|B_{\rho_{0}}(z)\setminus\Omega|\geq c_{0}|B_{\rho_{0}}|\geq c_{0}\left(\frac{\rho_{0}}{4\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)}\right)^{n}|B_{a\rho}|,

where the constant
\rho_{0}=\rho_{0}(\Omega)
is determined in (
2.1
). On the other hand, if
a\rho>4\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)
, then there is a point
y\in B_{a\rho}(z)
such that
B_{a\rho/8}(y)\subset B_{a\rho}(z)
but
B_{a\rho/8}(y)\cap\Omega=\emptyset
. Thus, we have

|B_{a\rho}(z)\setminus\Omega|>|B_{a\rho/8}(y)\setminus\Omega|>1/8^{n}|B_{a\rho}|

whenever
a\rho>4\mathrm{diam}(\Omega)
. Combining all the three cases, we have for any
a\geq 2

(2.31)

|B_{a\rho}(z)\setminus\Omega|>c|B_{a\rho}|,

where
c=c(n,\Omega)
.
Therefore, by
[
Coz17
, Corollary 4.9]
with
s
and
p
,
replaced by
s+\tau-2\tau/q
and
q
, respectively, we have for any
a\geq 2
,

(2.32)

\displaystyle\left(\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{B_{a\rho}(z)}|{w}|^{2}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq c(a\rho)^{s+\tau-2\tau/q}\left(\int_{B_{a\rho}(z)}\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{B_{a\rho}(z)}\frac{|{w}(x)-{w}(y)|^{q}}{|x-y|^{n+q(s+\tau)-2\tau}}\,dx\,dy\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\leq c(a\rho)^{2(s+\tau)}\left(\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{\mathcal{B}_{a\rho}(z)}|W|^{q}\,d\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

for some constant
c=c(\mathsf{data})
, where we have also used (
2.30
) for the last inequality. Similarly, as in the proof of
[
KMS15a
, Theorem 3.2]
together with a suitable choice of testing function
w\varphi^{2}
, where
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(B_{15\rho/8}(z))
with
\varphi\equiv 1
on
B_{7\rho/4}(z)
, we get

\displaystyle\rho^{-n+2s}[w]^{2}_{W^{s,2}(B_{\rho}(z))}

\displaystyle\leq\rho^{-n+2s}[w]^{2}_{W^{s,2}(B_{7\rho/4}(z))}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{\sigma}\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{B_{2\rho}(z)}|w|^{2}\,dx+\sigma\left(\rho^{2s}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{2\rho}(z)}\frac{|w|}{|y-z|^{n+2s}}\,dy\right)^{2}.

We next follow the same computations as in the proof of
[
KMS15a
, Proposition 4.4]
along with (
2.32
) to see that (
2.29
) holds.

Since we have a reverse Hölder’s type inequality on the diagonal as in (
2.29
), by following the same lines as in the proof of
[
KMS15a
, Theorem 1.3]
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left(\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{\mathcal{B}_{2R}}|W|^{2+\delta_{1}}\,d\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{2+\delta_{1}}}

\displaystyle\leq c\left[\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{-k(s-\tau)}\left(\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{B_{3R\times 2^{k}}}|W|^{2}\,d\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+R^{s-\tau}\left(\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{\mathcal{B}_{3R}}F^{2}\,d\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right]

for some constant
\delta_{1}=\delta_{1}(\mathsf{data})\in(0,1)
, where
c=c(\mathsf{data})
. After a few simple computations and using the fact that

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{-k(s-\tau)}\left(\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{B_{3R\times 2^{k}}}|W|^{2}\,d\mu\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq cr^{-n/2}[w]_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})},

we obtain

\displaystyle R^{s+\frac{n\delta_{0}}{2+2\delta_{0}}-\frac{n}{2}}[w]_{W^{s+\frac{n\delta_{0}}{2+2\delta_{0}},2+2\delta_{0}}(B_{2R})}

\displaystyle\leq cR^{-\frac{n}{2}}(R^{s}[w]_{W^{s,2}(B_{23R/8})}+\|w\|_{L^{2}(B_{23R/8})})

\displaystyle\quad+c\mathrm{Tail}(w;B_{5R/2}),

where
c=c(\mathsf{data})
. We now use standard energy inequalities as in
[
KMS15a
, Theorem 3.2]
to get the desired estimate (
2.28
).
∎

We end this section with the following local boundedness result up to the boundary.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
v\in W^{s,2}(B_{R}(x_{0}))\cap L^{1}_{2s}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
be a weak solution to

\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{L}_{A}v&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\Omega\cap B_{R}(x_{0})$},\\
v&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $B_{R}(x_{0})\setminus\Omega$}.\end{aligned}\right.

Then there is a constant
c=c(\mathsf{data})
such that

(2.33)

\|v\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{R/2}(x_{0}))}\leq c(R^{-n}\|v\|_{L^{1}(B_{R}(x_{0}))}+\mathrm{Tail}(v;B_{R}(x_{0}))).

Proof.

By
[
KKP16
, Theorem 2 and Theorem 5]
, we have

\|v\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{r/2}(z))}\leq c(r^{-n/2}\|v\|_{L^{2}(B_{r}(z))}+\mathrm{Tail}(v;B_{r}(z)))

for some constant
c=c(\mathsf{data})
, whenever
z\in\overline{\Omega}\cap B_{3R/4}(x_{0})
and
r\leq R/4
. Therefore, by following the same lines as the proof of
[
KMS15a
, Corollary 2.1]
, we get (
2.33
).
∎

3.
Comparison estimates

In this section, we provide several comparison estimates.

In light of the localization argument as in Lemma
2.3
, we may assume that
u\in W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
is a solution to

(3.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{L}_{A}u&=f&&\qquad\mbox{in $\Omega\cap B_{R}(x_{0})$},\\
u&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $B_{R}(x_{0})\setminus\Omega$},\end{aligned}\right.

where
f\in L^{2_{*}}(\Omega\cap B_{R}(x_{0}))
with
R\leq 1
.

Let us fix
B_{4r}(z)\Subset B_{R}(x_{0})
with
z\in\overline{\Omega}
. We present our first comparison estimate below.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
w\in W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
be the weak solution to

(3.2)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{L}_{A}w&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\Omega\cap B_{2r}(z)$},\\
w&=u&&\qquad\mbox{in $\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus(\Omega\cap B_{2r}(z))$}.\end{aligned}\right.

Then

(3.3)

\displaystyle r^{-n}[u-w]^{2}_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}\leq c\left(\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{\Omega\cap B_{2r}(z)}(r^{s}|f|)^{2_{*}}\,dx\right)^{\frac{2}{2_{*}}}

for some constant
c=c(\mathsf{data})
.

Proof.

By
[
BLS18
, Proposition 2.12]
, let
w\in X^{s,2}_{u}(\Omega\cap B_{2r}(z),B_{3r}(z))
be the weak solution to (
3.2
). Since
u\in W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
, we have
w\in W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
and
u-w\in X_{0}^{s,2}(\Omega\cap B_{2r}(z))
. Thus, by testing
u-w
to

\mathcal{L}_{A}(u-w)=f\quad\text{in }\Omega\cap B_{2r}(z),

we have

\displaystyle[u-w]^{2}_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}\leq c\int_{\Omega\cap B_{2r}(z)}|f||u-w|\,dx

where
c=c(\mathsf{data})
. We now use Young’s inequality and the Sobolev embedding as in
[
Coz17
, Lemma 4.8]
to obtain

(3.4)

\displaystyle r^{-n}[u-w]^{2}_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}\leq\frac{r^{-n}}{2}[u-w]^{2}_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}+c\left(\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{\Omega\cap B_{2r}(z)}(r^{s}|f|)^{2_{*}}\,dx\right)^{\frac{2}{2_{*}}}

for some constant
c=c(\mathsf{data})
, where we have used (
2.1
) to see that

r^{n}/c\leq|\Omega\cap B_{2r}(z)|\leq cr^{n}.

From (
3.4
), the conclusion of the lemma follows immediately.
∎

We next provide a second comparison estimate.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
v\in W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
be a weak solution to

(3.5)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{L}_{a_{0}}v&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\Omega\cap B_{r}(z)$},\\
v&=w&&\qquad\mbox{in ${\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus(\Omega\cap B_{r}(z))$},\end{aligned}\right.

where
a_{0}
is a kernel coefficient satisfying (
1.3
) and
w
is the solution to (
3.2
). Then, we have

(3.6)

\displaystyle r^{-n}[w-v]_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq c\big(r^{-2n}\|w/r^{s}\|^{2}_{L^{1}(B_{2r}(z))}+r^{-2s}\mathrm{Tail}(w;B_{2r}(z))^{2}\big)r^{-\frac{2n\delta_{0}}{1+\delta_{0}}}\|A-a_{0}\|_{L^{\frac{2(1+\delta_{0})}{\delta_{0}}}(B_{2r}(z)\times B_{2r}(z))}^{2}

\displaystyle+cr^{4s}\left(\sup_{x\in B_{r}(z)}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{2r}(z)}\frac{|A-a_{0}|}{|y-z|^{n+2s}}\,dy\right)^{2}\big(r^{-2n}\|w/r^{s}\|^{2}_{L^{1}(B_{2r}(z))}+r^{-2s}\mathrm{Tail}(w;B_{2r}(z))^{2}\big)

\displaystyle+cr^{2s}\left(\sup_{x\in B_{r}(z)}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{2r}(z)}\frac{|w(y)||A-a_{0}|}{|y-z|^{n+2s}}\,dy\right)^{2},

where the constant
\delta_{0}=\delta_{0}(\mathsf{data})\in(0,1)
is determined in Lemma
2.6
.

Proof.

We may assume
z=0
. By testing
w-v
to

\mathcal{L}_{A-a_{0}}w+\mathcal{L}_{a_{0}}(w-v)=0\quad\text{in }\Omega\cap B_{r},

we get

\displaystyle[w-v]_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq c\int_{B_{3r/2}}\int_{B_{3r/2}}\frac{|w(x)-w(y)||(w-v)(x)-(w-v)(y)||A-a_{0}|}{|x-y|^{n+2s}}\,dx\,dy

\displaystyle\quad+c\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{3r/2}}\int_{B_{r}}\frac{|w(x)-w(y)||(w-v)(x)||A-a_{0}|}{|x-y|^{n+2s}}\,dx\,dy\eqqcolon J_{1}+J_{2}.

With the help of Young’s inequality and Hölder’s inequality, we now estimate
J_{1}
as

\displaystyle J_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{[w-v]_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}}{8}+c\int_{B_{3r/2}}\int_{B_{3r/2}}\frac{|w(x)-w(y)|^{2}|A-a_{0}|^{2}}{|x-y|^{n+2s}}\,dx\,dy

\displaystyle\leq\frac{[w-v]_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}}{8}+c[w]^{2}_{W^{s+\frac{n\delta_{0}}{2+2\delta_{0}},2+2\delta_{0}}(B_{3r/2})}\left(\int_{B_{3r/2}}\int_{B_{3r/2}}|A-a_{0}|^{\frac{2(1+\delta_{0})}{\delta_{0}}}\,dx\,dy\right)^{\frac{\delta_{0}}{1+\delta_{0}}}

for some constant
c=c(\mathsf{data})
.
By Lemma
2.6
, we further simplify the above display as

(3.7)

\displaystyle J_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{[w-v]_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}}{8}+c\left(\|w\|^{2}_{L^{2}(B_{7r/4})}+r^{n-2s}\mathrm{Tail}(w;B_{7r/4})^{2}\right)r^{-\frac{2n\delta_{0}}{1+\delta_{0}}}\|A-a_{0}\|_{L^{\frac{2(1+\delta_{0})}{\delta_{0}}}(B_{2r}\times B_{2r})}^{2}

for some constant
c=c(\mathsf{data})
.
We next note
|x-y|\geq|y|/2
for any
y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{3r/2}
and
x\in B_{r}
. Thus,

\displaystyle J_{2}

\displaystyle\leq c\int_{B_{r}}|w(x)||(w-v)(x)|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{3r/2}}\frac{|A-a_{0}|}{|y|^{n+2s}}\,dx\,dy

\displaystyle\quad+c\int_{B_{r}}|(w-v)(x)|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{3r/2}}\frac{|w(y)||A-a_{0}|}{|y|^{n+2s}}\,dx\,dy\eqqcolon J_{2,1}+J_{2,2}.

We first estimate
J_{2,1}
as

\displaystyle J_{2,1}

\displaystyle\leq c\int_{B_{r}}|w(x)||(w-v)(x)|\int_{B_{2r}\setminus B_{3r/2}}\frac{|A-a_{0}|}{|y|^{n+2s}}\,dx\,dy

\displaystyle\quad+c\int_{B_{r}}|w(x)||(w-v)(x)|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{2r}}\frac{|A-a_{0}|}{|y|^{n+2s}}\,dx\,dy\coloneqq J_{2,1,1}+J_{2,1,2}.

By Young’s inequality, Hölder’s inequality and the Sobolev embedding, we have

\displaystyle J_{2,1,1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{r^{2s}}\int_{B_{r}}|w(x)|^{2}\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{B_{2r}}|A-a_{0}|^{2}\,dy\,dx+\frac{[w-v]_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}^{2}}{8}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{cr^{\frac{n\delta_{0}}{1+\delta_{0}}}}{r^{2s}}\|w\|^{2}_{L^{2+2\delta_{0}}(B_{r})}\left(\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{B_{2r}}\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{B_{2r}}|A-a_{0}|^{\frac{2(1+\delta_{0})}{\delta_{0}}}\,dy\,dx\right)^{\frac{\delta_{0}}{1+\delta_{0}}}+\frac{[w-v]_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}^{2}}{8}

\displaystyle\leq c[w]^{2}_{W^{s+\frac{n\delta_{0}}{2+2\delta_{0}},2+2\delta_{0}}(B_{r})}\left(\int_{B_{2r}}\int_{B_{2r}}|A-a_{0}|^{\frac{2(1+\delta_{0})}{\delta_{0}}}\,dx\,dy\right)^{\frac{\delta_{0}}{1+\delta_{0}}}+\frac{[w-v]_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}^{2}}{8},

where
c=c(\mathsf{data})
. Thus, as in the estimate of (
3.7
), we obtain

\displaystyle J_{2,1,1}\leq\frac{[w-v]^{2}_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}}{8}+c[\|w\|^{2}_{L^{2}(B_{7r/4})}+r^{n-2s}\mathrm{Tail}(w;B_{7r/4})^{2}]r^{-\frac{2n\delta_{0}}{1+\delta_{0}}}\|A-a_{0}\|_{L^{\frac{2(1+\delta_{0})}{\delta_{0}}}(B_{2r}\times B_{2r})}^{2}

for some constant
c=c(\mathsf{data})
.
Using Young’s inequality and the Sobolev embedding, we next estimate
J_{2,1,2}
as

\displaystyle J_{2,1,2}

\displaystyle\leq cr^{2s}\left(\sup_{x\in B_{r}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{2r}}\frac{|A-a_{0}|}{|y|^{n+2s}}\,dy\right)^{2}\|w\|^{2}_{L^{2}(B_{r})}+\frac{[w-v]^{2}_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}}{8}.

Similarly, we also have

\displaystyle J_{2,2}

\displaystyle\leq c\|w\|^{2}_{L^{2}(B_{7r/4})}r^{-\frac{2n\delta_{0}}{1+\delta_{0}}}\|A-a_{0}\|_{L^{\frac{2(1+\delta_{0})}{\delta_{0}}}(B_{2r}\times B_{2r})}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+cr^{2s+n}\left(\sup_{x\in B_{r}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{2r}}\frac{|A-a_{0}||w(y)|}{|y|^{n+2s}}\,dy\right)^{2}+\frac{[w-v]^{2}_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}}{4}.

By combining all the estimates
J_{1}
,
J_{2,1,1}

J_{2,1,2}
and
J_{2,2}
together with the fact that

\|w\|_{L^{2}(B_{7r/4})}^{2}+r^{n}\mathrm{Tail}(w;B_{7r/4})^{2}\leq c(r^{-n}\|w\|^{2}_{L^{1}(B_{2r})}+r^{n}\mathrm{Tail}(w;B_{2r})^{2}),

which follows from Lemma
2.7
, we get the desired estimate (
3.6
).
∎

4.
The case of discontinuous coefficients

In this section, we prove Theorem
1.4
.
Let us fix a weak solution
u\in W^{s,2}(B_{1})\cap L^{1}_{2s}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
to (
1.5
). By the localization argument, we see the following remark.

Remark 4.1
.

By Remark
2.4
, we observe that there is a weak solution
\widetilde{u}\in W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
to

(4.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{L}_{{A}}\widetilde{u}&=f+g_{2}&&\qquad\mbox{in $\Omega\cap B_{3/4}$},\\
\widetilde{u}&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $B_{3/4}\setminus\Omega$},\\
\widetilde{u}&=u&&\qquad\mbox{in $B_{15/16}$},\end{aligned}\right.

where

(4.2)

\|g_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{3/4})}\leq c\mathrm{Tail}(u;B_{15/16}))

for some constant
c=c(n,s,\Lambda)
.

4.1.
Localized problems.

In this subsection, on account of Remark
4.1
, we fix a weak solution
u\in W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
to

\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{L}_{{A}}{u}&=f&&\qquad\mbox{in $\Omega\cap B_{3/4}$},\\
{u}&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $B_{3/4}\setminus\Omega$}.\end{aligned}\right.

We first provide the following weighted Hölder estimates for homogeneous problems with a locally constant kernel coefficient.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
z\in{\Omega}\cap B_{1/4}
and
r\in(0,1/64]
. Let
v\in W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
be a weak solution to

(4.3)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{L}_{a_{0}}v&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $\Omega\cap B_{r}(z)$},\\
v&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $B_{r}(z)\setminus\Omega$},\end{aligned}\right.

where

(4.4)

\displaystyle a_{0}(x,y)\coloneqq\begin{cases}(A)_{B_{2r}(z)}&\quad\text{if }(x,y)\in B_{2r}(z)\times B_{2r}(z),\\
A(x,y)&\quad\text{if }(x,y)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus(B_{2r}(z)\times B_{2r}(z)).\end{cases}

Then we have

r^{s}\|v/d^{s}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega\cap B_{r/4}(z))}\leq c\big(r^{-n}\|v\|_{L^{1}(B_{r}(z))}+\mathrm{Tail}(v;B_{r}(z))\big)

for some constant
c=c(\mathsf{data})
.

Proof.

We may assume
z=0
. With the help of Lemma
2.7
, we first observe that

(4.5)

\|v\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{3r/4})}\leq c(\|v\|_{L^{1}(B_{r})}+\mathrm{Tail}(v;B_{r}))

for some constant
c=c(\mathsf{data})
. By following the same lines as in the proof of Lemma
2.3
with
A(x,y)=a_{0}(x,y)
,
x_{0}=z
,
R=r
and
a=(A)_{B_{2r}(z)\times B_{2r}(z)}
, we see that there is a weak solution
\widetilde{v}
to

\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{L}_{\widetilde{A}}\widetilde{v}&=g_{1}+g_{2}&&\qquad\mbox{in $\Omega\cap B_{r/2}$},\\
\widetilde{v}&=0&&\qquad\mbox{in $B_{r/2}\setminus\Omega$},\\
\widetilde{v}&=v&&\qquad\mbox{in $B_{5r/8}$}.\end{aligned}\right.

In addition, we get

\widetilde{A}(x,y)=(A)_{B_{2r}(z)\times B_{2r}(z)}

and

(4.6)

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in B_{r/2}}(|g_{1}(x)|+|g_{2}(x)|)

\displaystyle\leq cr^{-2s}\left[\sup_{x\in B_{r/2}}|v(x)|+\mathrm{Tail}(v;B_{5r/8})\right]

\displaystyle\leq cr^{-2s}(r^{-n}\|v\|_{L^{1}(B_{r})}+\mathrm{Tail}(v;B_{r})),

where we have used (
4.5
).
In light of
[
FR24
, Proposition 2.7.8]
along with (
4.5
), (
4.6
) and the fact that
\widetilde{v}\equiv 0
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus B_{3r/4}
and
|\widetilde{v}(x)|\leq|v(x)|
, we have

\displaystyle r^{s}\|\widetilde{v}/d^{s}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega\cap B_{r/4})}

\displaystyle\leq c(\|\widetilde{v}\|_{L^{\infty}(B_{3r/4})}+r^{2s}\|g_{1}+g_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega\cap B_{r/2})})

\displaystyle\leq c(r^{-n}\|v\|_{L^{1}(B_{r})}+\mathrm{Tail}(v;B_{r})),

where
c=c(\mathsf{data})
. Since
\widetilde{v}\equiv v
in
B_{5r/8}
, the desired estimate follows.
∎

By considering two cases
d(z)\leq 2r
and
d(z)>2r
, we simply obtain the following inequality

(4.7)

\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{B_{r}(z)}|g|/r^{s}\,dx\leq c\max\{1,{d^{s}(z)}/{r^{s}}\}\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{B_{r}(z)}|g|/d^{s}\,dx

for some constant
c=c(n,s)
. We note that this inequality will be frequently used in the remaining parts.

Using comparison estimates given in Section
3
along with Lemma
4.2
, we have the following result.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
z\in{\Omega}\cap B_{1/4}
and
r\in(0,1/64]
. For any
\varepsilon>0
, there is a constant
\delta=\delta(\mathsf{data},\varepsilon)\in(0,1)
such that if

(4.8)

\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{\Omega\cap B_{4r}(z)}|u/d^{s}|\,dx+\max\{d(z),4r\}^{-s}\mathrm{Tail}(u;B_{4r}(z))\leq\lambda

and

(4.9)

\lambda\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{B_{2r}(z)}\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{B_{2r}(z)}|A-(A)_{B_{2r}(z)\times B_{2r}(z)}|\,dx\,dy+\left(\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{\Omega\cap B_{2r}(z)}(r^{s}|f|)^{2_{*}}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{2_{*}}}\leq\delta\lambda,

then there is a weak solution
v
to

\mathcal{L}_{a_{0}}v=0\quad\text{in }\Omega\cap B_{r}(z),

where
a_{0}
is as defined in (
4.4
), satisfying

\|v/d^{s}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega\cap B_{r/4}(z))}\leq c\lambda\quad\text{and}\quad\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{\Omega\cap B_{r}(z)}|u/d^{s}-v/d^{s}|\,dx\leq c\varepsilon\lambda

for some constant
c=c(\mathsf{data})
.

Proof.

Let
w
and
v
be the weak solutions to (
3.2
) and (
3.5
), respectively. By Lemma
3.1
and Lemma
3.2
together with the fact that
A(x,y)=a_{0}(x,y)
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}\setminus(B_{2r}(z)\times B_{2r}(z))
, we get

\displaystyle r^{-n}[u-v]^{2}_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}\leq 2r^{-n}[u-w]^{2}_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}+2r^{-n}[w-v]^{2}_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}

\displaystyle\leq c\left(\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{\Omega\cap B_{2r}(z)}(r^{s}|f|)^{2_{*}}\,dx\right)^{\frac{2}{2_{*}}}

\displaystyle\quad+c\big(r^{-2n}\|w/r^{s}\|^{2}_{L^{1}(B_{2r}(z))}+r^{-2s}\mathrm{Tail}(w;B_{2r}(z))^{2}\big)r^{-\frac{2n\delta_{0}}{1+\delta_{0}}}\|A-a_{0}\|_{L^{\frac{2(1+\delta_{0})}{\delta_{0}}}(B_{2r}(z)\times B_{2r}(z))}^{2}

for some constant
c=c(\mathsf{data})
.
We note from Hölder’s inequality, the Sobolev embedding and Lemma
3.1
that

\displaystyle r^{-2n}\|w/r^{s}\|^{2}_{L^{1}(B_{2r}(z))}

\displaystyle\leq c\left(r^{-2n}\|(w-u)/r^{s}\|^{2}_{L^{1}(B_{2r}(z))}+r^{-2n}\|u/r^{s}\|^{2}_{L^{1}(B_{2r}(z))}\right)

\displaystyle\leq c\left(r^{-n}[w-u]_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}^{2}+r^{-2n}\|u/r^{s}\|^{2}_{L^{1}(B_{2r}(z))}\right)

\displaystyle\leq c\left[\left(\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{\Omega\cap B_{2r}(z)}(r^{s}|f|)^{2_{*}}\,dx\right)^{\frac{2}{2_{*}}}+r^{-2n}\|u/r^{s}\|^{2}_{L^{1}(B_{2r}(z))}\right].

Combining the above two inequalities along with (
4.9
), we get

(4.10)

\displaystyle r^{-n}[u-v]^{2}_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}

\displaystyle\leq c\left[(\delta\lambda)^{2}+\delta^{\frac{\delta_{0}}{1+\delta_{0}}}(r^{-2n}\|u/r^{s}\|^{2}_{L^{1}(B_{2r}(z))}+r^{-2s}\mathrm{Tail}(u;B_{2r}(z))^{2})\right].

We now estimate the left-hand side of the above inequality by considering two cases that
d(z)\leq 2r
or
d(z)>2r
.

We first assume
d(z)\leq 2r
. Then we observe from (
4.7
) and (
4.8
) that

(4.11)

\displaystyle r^{-n}\|u/r^{s}\|_{L^{1}(B_{2r}(z))}+r^{-s}\mathrm{Tail}(u;B_{2r}(z))

\displaystyle\leq c\left(\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{\Omega\cap B_{4r}(z)}|u/d^{s}|\,dx+\max\{d(z),r\}^{-s}\mathrm{Tail}(u;B_{4r}(z))\right)\leq c\lambda.

In addition, by Hölder’s inequality together with (
2.1
) and Lemma
2.5
, we have

r^{-2n}\|u/d^{s}-v/d^{s}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega\cap B_{r}(z))}\leq cr^{-n}\|u/d^{s}-v/d^{s}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega\cap B_{r}(z))}\leq cr^{-n}[u-v]^{2}_{W^{s,2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})}.

Therefore, we have

(4.12)

\mathop{}\mkern-3.0mu\mathchoice{\hbox to0.0pt{$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\textstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.83331pt{\hss$\scriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to5.1215pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox to4.82639pt{\hss$\scriptscriptstyle{-}$\hss}}$\hss}}\mkern-3.0mu\int_{\Omega\cap B_{r}(z)}|u/d^{s}-v/d^{s}|\,dx\leq c\left(\delta\lambda+\delta^{\frac{\delta_{0}}{2(1+\delta_{0})}}\lambda\right)\leq c\varepsilon\lambda

by taking
\delta
sufficiently small depending only on
\mathsf{data}
and
\varepsilon
. In addition, from Lemma
4.2
, the Sobolev embedding and Lemma
3.1
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\|v/d^{s}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega\cap B_{r/4}(z))}

\displaystyle\leq c(r^{-n}\|v/r^{s}\|_{L^{1}(B_{r}(z))}+\mathrm{Tail}(v/r^{s};B_{r}(z)))
